ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e3923	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e3938	Ladies and gentlemen, to clarify the position once again – I have already done so in the main committee today –: In our government programme, we have determined that Austria is pursuing the goal of pursuing a tailor-made partnership with Turkey. That, as I believe, for good reason, because we also need cooperation with Turkey on many issues. Turkey is an important energy hub. It is certainly a certain safety pad also towards the Middle East, and it is for many also a market for the future, also for Austrian companies. That is why we see as an objective of Austrian foreign policy to move closer to a tailor-made partnership. You are right that the negotiations are de facto on ice, because eight chapters, which have very strong implications for the free movement of the European Union, are currently not visible in Turkey. We expect a new progress report from the Commission in the autumn of this year, but I can tell you that nobody in the European Union is actually expecting a movement from Turkey on these eight key issues. It is the question of how this will work in the future. That is why we need to be careful here, carefully in the sense that we take into account the key interests of the future market, the energy hub, also security issues, and at some point bring them into a common partnership. And that, I believe, is also the objective of Austrian foreign policy.

ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e8328	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, who are watching us in plenary! This is the first equal treatment report we are dealing with here in Parliament, which is not only about equal treatment between women and men, but also about anti-discrimination and equal treatment, no matter what ethnicity, religion, belief in the world, what age or what sexual orientation someone has. There was a change in the equal treatment law in 2004. This had to be done, among other things, because the EU, with two anti-discrimination directives, required that discrimination in the working world for the above reasons is not acceptable and that it should also be prosecuted and persecuted, and that discrimination and inequality due to ethnicity should also be unacceptable and prosecuted in other areas – in terms of services and access to goods. One of the problems we have is that we have a minimum to minimal implementation of these two EU directives. My colleague Judith Schwentner has already raised the big problem that in certain discrimination statistics, protection against discrimination continues, for example in the case of ethnicity, and other discrimination statistics, such as discrimination due to sexual orientation, have not been given as much protection as in the former group. This is definitely not acceptable. If we as a society say that discrimination is not a cavalier’s offence and that discrimination must not be tolerated, discrimination for whatever reason, then it is not understandable and not to see why certain victim groups or certain groups of groups affected by discrimination should be better or different from other victim groups. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] That is why the Greens also said in 2004 that this needs to be repaired, that discrimination protection must apply equally to all groups, and that is what we are saying to this day, and we are still seeing this need for change. A second major problem that we have in the fight against discrimination in our country is the sharp human under-employment of the equal treatment prosecution. The Court of Justice has in 2004 been given new tasks, many, namely to combat discrimination for reasons of ethnicity, religion, belief, age and sexual orientation. However, the staff and the resources of the equal treatment prosecution were hardly increased. In other words, almost the same equal treatment attorneyship now has to deal with much more tasks. And this becomes a major problem: that victims and victims of discrimination, people who are I do not see why I am being discriminated against, I am looking for my rights, I want to enforce my right, unfortunately, in many cases because of the scarce human resources cannot be advised and unfortunately abandoned. A third major problem is that the victims of discrimination with the process and cost risk are largely left alone. If someone says, "No, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, I go to court and I want to sue my right!, and is not very good and cannot afford to a lawyer or lawyer and then lose the trial in court, then this person not only has to bear the trial costs of himself, but also the costs of the opposite. And you can imagine that in many cases this leads to victims of discrimination saying: No, I'd rather not take that risk, because that could be very expensive for me! – In other words, the fear of having to be held financially by themselves is still being felt to the detriment and discrimination that have been suffered. If we as legislators of our Republic say that discrimination must not be allowed, discrimination is unacceptable, then we should also be consistent enough to support those people who are affected by discrimination – whether they are women, men, older people, people with other sexual orientation, minority members – with resources so that they can really enforce their right. Unfortunately, this is still not the case. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] Finally, I would like to mention three brief examples from the report so that we all know what specific cases are in everyday life: A 48-year-old woman applied for a serving job. The interview will be terminated after she has correctly answered the question after her age, namely that she is 48 years old. The reason that is called is: The management has the requirement to stop anyone who is over 35 years old. How does a woman – or, of course, a man – come to be excluded only because of his or her age from an application or from a job? Second example: An Austrian calls for her husband for a job at the local waste disposal. When asked where her husband came from, she answered truthfully: from Africa. – The answer she receives is – I quote –: We don't need that here, and the lady on the phone is already on the phone. I'm asking all of us: How does a person who lives here legally and is allowed to work here not even be admitted to an interview, just because of his skin color? [[Congratulation with the greens.]] Third example: A man works as a salesman in a shoe shop and exits at his workplace as a homosexual. After a year he is informed by the branch management that the company wants to terminate him because he – quote – is “too extravagant”. – Of course, this is also discrimination and just as negative. Ladies and gentlemen, it should not remain with lip confessions. If we say that discrimination is unacceptable, we are fighting as a society for equal treatment for all those living in that country, then we should also take the consequences, improve the equal treatment law, and also equip the equal treatment prosecutions with means so that they can really support discrimination. - Thank you.

Point Report by the Committee on Research, Innovation and Technology on amendment 652/A by Mr Mag. Karin Hakl, Ing. Kurt Gartlehner, colleagues on a federal law amending the Telecommunications Act 2003 (TKG 2003) (212 d.b.)
ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e9495	We're coming to the 2nd. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. I shall be the first speaker to give Mrs Magistrate. Hakl the word. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e9522	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! High house! I am really pleased that today we can present a novel to the TKG, of which the “FORMAT” has written: A little law, a big throw. In this law, we foresee that in the future all telecommunications lines can be used by all telecommunications service providers, that management rights are granted, which make it possible for a Telekom company to be able to move its lines anywhere in Austria in every private home, through public reason. What does that mean? – Finally, one can use glass fibres that have already been moved in empty shafts, in empty tubes, for example, from an energy company that is not used up, even for telecommunications services. Those electricity companies or small Telekom providers, who have not yet reached large surface coverage, can go to the networks of those who have a large surface coverage and offer their products. We quickly come to a large and comprehensive, powerful network in this way, and that was overdue. We are, in particular, very far behind the expansion of glass fibre. Broadband has not been broadband for a long time! Assuming someone has a 2-megabit connection that a child plays, someone might want to watch the computer or call YouTube, and the father wants to send e-mails – you can't do that with 2 megabits, because you need more powerful, bigger bandwidths. And this law makes it possible for the necessary investments to be efficient and quickly possible. We have also reduced the time of the proceedings by half. That is to say, the decision to expand something immediately also draws the opportunity to actually make the investment, and I really think that is a big throw that we need very urgently. We must not conclude that we still have regions in rural areas which are not yet provided with broadband at all – not too many, but after all – and we do not have to give in to illusion, to believe, This fibre-optic investment is now going to advance to the limits in a very short time until the lower corner of Austria. – We need other solutions. For example, it would be economically important and useful to allocate the so-called digital dividend to mobile communications. The digital dividend is the frequency range that has become free from analogous to digital by changing television. Digital television now needs much less bandwidth, and this popular capacity of existing frequencies could be written out and given to mobile phones. This would have the advantage of expanding the mobile network significantly more efficiently – with fewer masts, with a greater range, with greater penetration effect – so that there would be really significant bandwidths available in the mobile area throughout Austria within a short time. I know that's not easy. In this frequency range, for example, the radio microphones of the Bregenzer Festspiele send. There must be solutions that could be used to finance a changeover, but I can imagine that this would be possible from the auction proceeds of these frequencies without major problems. There are other conditions to be considered, but I believe that we should not wait until 2015, but I would ask you, Mrs Federal Minister, to convene a round table with all those concerned as soon as possible in order to come to solutions as quickly as possible. Others, such as the Federal Republic of Germany, like France, like the United Kingdom, have already made this decision and will devote these frequencies to mobile communications for precisely the reasons I have mentioned – because of the great economic benefit that is generated, because information is no longer just going through television and radio, but it is very much over the Internet. In addition to the use of lines and the introduction of management rights, this amendment also includes other important measures in the field of competition: It has been set down by law how markets can be redefined, because it is clear that the telephonery of competition is no longer only in the fixed network sector. If I am still the market leader at 10 percent of fixed network customers at some point, because everyone else is calling mobile for a long time, that means that markets have long been redefined. This has already been done in the past, we create the legal foundations and framework conditions more or less afterwards. That too, I think, was important. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] In autumn, we will have a new innovation in the house that will create new conditions for competition – the Federal Minister will present a government proposal in this regard. We have preferred more or less points in this joint initiative proposal, which now, as the crisis is at its peak, allow for rapid investment. So we will be able to further deepen these topics in the autumn. Thank you.

Or under 4.4.2.1., Chapter institutional system, Foundations: 'Since the Council is able to take only a few decisions autonomously, but generally to adopt a legal act on the basis of a draft of the Commission, the Commission's own-initiative role is crucial.' The Commission is therefore sometimes referred to as the Community's engine. But, in the same paragraph, all this is, of course, only realistic on the basis of the possible enforceable in the Council. . That is to say, the European Parliament, the Council and the Commission are described as the three essential elements of the decision-making process in the European Union. It is shown that it comes to the really sensible and wise balance. But, and this is crucial, the President of the Commission must present himself to the European Parliament and will, of course, also have to address the issues, issues and priorities expressed by the individual political groups and Members. ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e14610	Madam President. Mr. Federal Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen! However, on the part of the ÖVP, the right to speak by Austrian MEPs in the National Council plenary session was by no means rejected. I pointed out in the Committee on the Rules of Procedure that this right of speech is very likely for us and how it should be designed. We believe that the following three points should be taken into account: Firstly, there should be this right of speech only during the current hours of Europe and not also during the debates on the declarations of Europe by members of the government. Secondly, the equal treatment of all Austrian MEPs must be ensured. That is to say, not only the speakers sent by the clubs, but also the representatives appointed by a candidate party in accordance with the European electoral order, must be taken into account accordingly. Otherwise, all Austrian MEPs in the EU's main committee and the corresponding subcommittee would have a right to speak, but only those Members of the European Union who belong to the club would have a right to speak in plenary. This would, of course, mean that the representatives of the Martin list in plenary would have no right to speak. In the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, we have clearly expressed our view against such inconsistencies that are not justified. Thirdly, we also agree that opinions from EU Commissioners and other EU representatives should continue to be possible only in the EU's main committee and in informal plenary sessions. It must be taken into account that the Commissioners are responsible to the European Parliament and not to the national parliaments. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen, as you know and as is usual, changes in the Rules of Procedure in Parliament should be carried out on a broad basis. This broad basis was not in sight in the last Committee on the Rules of Procedure, and therefore we have agreed to the compromise proposed by Madam President Prammer. Should a broad consensus still be possible, however, we are willing to agree to the introduction of a right of speech for all Austrian MEPs within the framework of "Current European Hours". Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2009-06-17-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00027_d7e20076	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Mr President of the Court of Auditors! Ladies and gentlemen, I will be looking at the Court of Auditors' report on the measures to be taken. The Commission's proposal for a Council Directive on the approximation of the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to the protection of the environment and health at work in the construction of roads in Austria with regard to the impact on costs. This review had already taken place at the end of 2006. Selected road construction projects were tested. I may mention some: It was the A 8, Innkreis motorway between Sattledt and Wels, the S 31, Burgenland-Schnellstraße, the S 1/A 5, North motorway, and the S 33, Kremser expressway, which is still in the planning phase. It should be noted that the share of environmental and health costs in road construction has significantly increased over the past 20 years. 28 percent of total costs were spent on the projects under review. The main part of these costs was noise protection and environmental compensation. In the case of noise protection, it should be noted that the projects under study cost up to 32 percent of the total cost, which is also due to the fact that the lowest emission limits in Europe are applied in Austria. The costs of the individual construction measures clearly vary greatly. The costs are particularly high where the use of tunnels is required. It is precisely the disappearance of the underground road, however, that increases the acceptance of the people, because 29 percent of our population feel disturbed by the noise. In the years 2000 to 2006, ASFINAG built 380 km of noise protection walls along the existing motorways and highways and invested 246 million €. Significant sums have also been spent on the ecological compensatory funds. Measures such as wild crossing aid, amphibian protection facilities or replacement forestry were prescribed to limit the negative impact of road construction projects on the ecological structure of road constructions. The cost of these eco-measures was around 10 percent of the total cost of these construction projects. Measures have also been taken to protect groundwater and to combat air pollution. Ladies and gentlemen, what does the Court of Auditors say about this? – It points out that noise protection measures such as the construction of tunnels, underfloors or the housing of roads significantly increase the cost of construction and thus the new road construction in the urban area is at its economic limits. That is why he makes recommendations which I can only inform you of. The assessment of the still existing need for noise protection facilities has been addressed. This is the result of future construction programmes. However, it is also intended to develop guidelines for an evaluation system that clarify the benefits-cost ratio to which a road construction project is useful for both economic and economic purposes. This would also give rise to priority lines. In addition, directives and instructions should be drawn up, in particular, for those ecological compensation measures that cause high costs. I would like to add one more point: As far as transport planning is concerned, more attention should be paid to valuable natural areas. Ladies and gentlemen! The Court of Auditors' report shows that road construction has often reached the limits of financial viability. Without long-term planning and early integration of spatial planning, new buildings will be difficult to finance in the light of environmental and health protection. – Thank you very much.

Our report on measures to protect the environment and health in road construction in Austria was also addressed here. This is where the Court of Auditors has carried out an audit because, just over the last decades, costs in these areas have increased considerably. In the previous debate, it was argued that the projects under review were spent on average 28 percent on these measures, more than 30 percent on noise protection, almost 18 percent on environmental compensation, 2.6 percent on water protection and 14 percent on land. A review has shown that there is also an improvement potential, a cost reduction potential, and in this context I would now like to address the issue you have raised, noise protection measures, where there have undoubtedly been problems, but still cannot be attributed to the ASFINAG of the Black Peter, because noise protection measures are precisely mandatory and therefore to be implemented accordingly. And for this area, there are no binding limits – neither national nor international – but there are guidelines by the WHO, which have been taken into account or implemented to varying degrees. A problem in this area, for example, is that there are no directives or service instructions in respect of environmental compensation measures, except for the protection of animals, and so, of course, there is a risk that without directives, measures will be imposed without service instructions, causing unnecessary costs. The same problem in the field of water protection, where there are no limit values, and also as often as often as costs that could be avoided, could be specified in this respect. A problem is also the control of the implementation of action: There has also been no action, although there is a legal obligation to do so. And it should also be taken into account that in this area, for example, the approval documents already contain requirements on this subject. The Brenner Base Tunnel was also mentioned several times in this debate. I would like to point out that the Court of Auditors is well aware that from 1994 to 2007 there has been a strong increase in truck traffic, especially on the A13. We are also aware that studies and forecasts set out the need to shift traffic from road to rail. The Brenner Base Tunnel is, in any case, a project which, on the one hand, demands increased from the construction engineering sector, as well as, of course, in terms of transport policy requirements, but at the same time will create enormous financial challenges towards the budget in the coming years. In 2002, for example, in terms of construction costs, this amounted to € 4.5 billion, in 2007 at €6 billion – and according to cost estimate 2009 at €8 billion. The construction costs for the Brenner-Base Tunnel project – with the expected completion end in 2025 – are expected to amount to approximately 9.8 billion, with no financing costs. If the financing costs are involved, the pre-valorization is based on this, then that means that only on the Austrian side, costs of € 12 billion are expected to be on the budget or on the ÖBB. It must be said that, of course, a part can be financed cross-financed, especially from toll receipts on the A13, but it is already stated that in 2006 toll revenue was only achieved in the amount of approximately € 21.48 million, i.e. a very small amount. I would also like to point out that more than € 780 million has been made available from EU funds for the period 2007 to 2013, with about € 383 million being spent by 50 percent in Austria. In other words, the expected cost of €12 billion will cover a small part of cross-financing or EU funding; the rest will have to be covered by grants to be provided by the public authorities. That is why the Court of Auditors has pointed out that we need a binding financing concept so that a project that is being launched can also be completed financially. [[Abg. Dr. Moser: There's no such thing as that!]] There is a need for an extension of the access and follow-up routes, also on the German and Italian sides – that was addressed by Minister Bures – so that the shift from road to rail is attractive. For a successful operation, therefore, important conditions are needed. With regard to construction preparation, as regards implementation and concrete construction measures, anti-claming strategies, as established by the Court of Auditors, should be implemented. There is also a need for a binding commitment that EU funds will actually flow for this, beyond 2013. The international commitments should be made as concrete as possible or should be taken into account in order to ensure, at all events, that civil law is safeguarded. I would like to express my express praise to Mrs Bundesminister that with the Memorandum of Understanding, which took place on 18 June.
